```
tiresias anchor                                   # v0.8: upgrade pending batches, then anchor everything new in one batch (W-08)
tiresias anchor status                            # v0.8: the setting, calendars, not-yet-anchored count, batches
tiresias anchor upgrade                           # v0.8: only ask whether pending batches reached a Bitcoin block
tiresias receipts prove <id> [--out <folder>]     # v0.8: a proof bundle for one receipt (record_fingerprint) or snapshot (id)
tiresias receipts verify <bundle>                 # v0.8: check a bundle offline; never says proven
```

Notes:

- `log` prints a readable markdown page (not JSON). `--to <folder>` writes one file, `tiresias-log-YYYY-MM-DD.md`, into a folder that must already exist, and says so if it replaced an earlier export of the same day.
- `TIRESIAS_PRIVATE=1` makes a whole session private (for example `TIRESIAS_PRIVATE=1 tiresias mcp`). `tiresias mcp --private` is the same as `TIRESIAS_PRIVATE=1 tiresias mcp` (since v0.8, integration Codex round 3; before, the flag was ignored). Since v0.8 (integration Codex rounds 1 and 2) a private session also refuses every owner command that would write or change something: `clear` (except `--dry-run`), `log --to`, `keys init`, `keys sign-directory --out`, `receipts prove --out`, `saved delete`, `anchor` and `anchor upgrade`, as it already refused slips, bookmarks, scanner, translator, settings and profile changes.
- `clear` trims history, receipts and cached pages to the limits in `config.json` (defaults: history 90 days, receipts 90 days, page cache 30 days, store cap 200 MB). `--older-than 30d` (whole days only) overrides all three age limits for that run. `--all` removes all history, receipts and cached pages. `--dry-run` reports exactly what a real run would remove and changes nothing. `clear` only deletes whole files and never touches `cooldowns.jsonl`.
- `needs` prints a readable markdown page, like `log`. Default window: the last 30 days.
- `watch` without `--page` checks every watched bookmark (for scheduled runs); with `--page N` it checks only the Nth group of 10, like the MCP tool. Tiresias has no scheduler. The owner schedules it, for example with cron: `0 7 * * * node /path/to/tiresias/dist/cli.js watch --text >> ~/tiresias-watch.log 2>&1` (on macOS, `crontab -e` works, or a launchd agent running the same command).
- `clear` (even `--all`) never touches `bookmarks/`, and the disk cap does not count it.
- `clear` and the daily trim delete handoff tickets past `keep_handoff_days` (default 7), one whole file each; `--older-than` applies to them too, and `--all` removes every ticket. The disk cap never removes an open ticket.
- `resume --from-file` takes a saved HTML, markdown, text or PDF file (PDF by its `%PDF-` start; otherwise the extension, else the content, decides). It must be a regular file no larger than the fetch cap (5 MB, `TIRESIAS_MAX_BYTES`). Nothing is sent to the site.
- Several agents share one store by pointing at the same folder: the default `~/.tiresias`, or the same `TIRESIAS_HOME` for each. The store must not sit in a synced folder, and sharing across machines is not in v0.3.
- Safe Browsing (W-19, v0.3): `config.json` takes `safe_browsing` (true or false, default true) and `web_risk_key_file` (a full path; default `~/.config/tiresias/secrets.env`). `TIRESIAS_SAFE_BROWSING=0` turns it off for a session. A Web Risk key comes from `TIRESIAS_WEB_RISK_KEY`, else the line `WEB_RISK_API_KEY=...` in the key file. `TIRESIAS_SAFETY_LIST_URL` and `TIRESIAS_WEB_RISK_ENDPOINT` exist only for the test suite and accept only this machine (host exactly 127.0.0.1, ::1 or localhost, http or https); anything else fails the call and nothing is sent. `safety update` is refused in private mode.
- Profiles (W-18, v0.4): the profile is chosen once per process: `--profile <name>`, else `TIRESIAS_PROFILE` (empty counts as unset), else `default`. A bad name is refused with fixed text that does not repeat it; a profile that does not exist stops the command before anything is read or created. `--profile` is refused on the `profile` commands themselves (they name the profile after the command). `profile create`, `profile remove` are refused in private mode. Every JSON output of a non-default profile carries top-level `profile`; `keys directory` prints only the directory JSON to host, unchanged.
- Each profile reads its own `config.json` (`<root>/profiles/<name>/config.json`; for `default`, the root's), so limits, `signature_agent`, `safe_browsing` and `web_risk_key_file` are per profile. The default Web Risk key file (`~/.config/tiresias/secrets.env`) is used by `default` only; `TIRESIAS_WEB_RISK_KEY` applies to whatever profile the process serves.
- Permission slips (W-05, v0.4): `slip grant` and `slip revoke` are refused in private mode; `slip list` reads only. They act on the current profile (`--profile`). `--until` takes a date (through the end of that local day) or a time (`2026-10-24T17:00`, optionally with `Z` or an offset); `--for` takes whole days or hours (`7d`, `12h`); without either a slip lasts 24 hours. `--note` is at most 200 characters on one line.
- Forms (W-04, v0.4): `--set name=value` gives one value; repeat a name for several checkbox or multiple-select values; `--unset name` chooses none for a checkbox group or a multiple select (a radio group cannot be emptied). `form submit` takes `--preview` with the hash the preview printed and needs the same `--form` and values. `form submit` is refused in private mode; `form preview` works privately. Both exit 1 unless the status is `ok`.
- Scanner (W-21, v0.5): `scanner set` writes `config.json` key `scanner` (`command`: a full path, no `..`, then up to 31 arguments, each at most 1,024 characters with no control characters; `timeout_seconds` 1 to 60, default 10; `send_file`, default false), keeping every other key, atomically, mode 0600; it checks the program file exists. `scanner set` and `scanner off` are refused in private mode. A scanner setting that does not validate is not run: file warnings say `scan: { verdict: "failed", by: "config.json" }`. Nothing in the MCP server reads or writes it. `TIRESIAS_MAX_FILE_BYTES` (default 50 MB) caps what is hashed (W-20). `clear` and the daily trim delete quarantine copies older than 10 minutes (left by a process that stopped mid-call) and report how many in `quarantine`.
- `config.json` (in `~/.tiresias/`, optional): `keep_history_days`, `keep_receipts_days`, `keep_cache_days`, `max_disk_mb` (each a whole number of 1 or more), and for signing `signature_agent` (the origin where your key directory is hosted, like `https://example.com`: no path, query, credentials or fragment) and `signature_agent_style` (`quoted`, the default, or `dictionary`). v0.3 adds `keep_handoff_days` (whole number of 1 or more, default 7). A bad file is a clear error from `clear`; a read never fails because of it.

- Network safety (v0.5.1): Tiresias calls ports 80, 443 and 8000 to 8999 on public addresses; `TIRESIAS_ALLOW_PORTS` adds more (`8080,9000-9100`). Other ports are refused with a message starting `port_blocked:`. An https page that redirects to plain http is refused (`downgrade:`). Redirects are followed only on 301, 302, 303, 307 and 308. Signing headers are sent only over https. `TIRESIAS_ALLOW_PRIVATE` is honored only under Node's test runner.

- Permission slips (v0.5.1): `slip grant` and `slip revoke` print what they are about to do to standard error and wait up to 2 minutes for the person to type the site back (`revoke all` for `--all`); input that is not a terminal is refused with fixed text and exit 1. `slips/revoked.jsonl` lists revoked ids.

- Politeness (v0.5.1): requests to one site start at least 1 second apart (more when its robots.txt sets a Crawl-delay), shared by every Tiresias process on the machine through `hosts/` in the store; a first read of a new site takes a few seconds while its discovery probes are spaced.


- Browse and settings (v0.6): `tiresias tabs [--page 2]`, `tiresias tabs close <n> | --all`, `tiresias tab <n>`, `tiresias back`, `tiresias forward` (each with `--section`, `--question`, `--page`); `tiresias files [--site] [--since] [--until] [--page]`; `tiresias settings` (also `show`/`list`), `tiresias settings set <name> [<site>] <value>`, `tiresias settings reset <name> [<site>]`. Settings take effect for new requests only; calls already running finish under the settings they started with.
- Rendering and translation (v0.7): `rendering` (`off`, the default; `ask`; `auto`) is set with `tiresias settings set rendering ...` or `config.json`, and `TIRESIAS_RENDERING` overrides it for a session; `TIRESIAS_CHROME` names the Chrome program (a full path), else the usual install places are searched; with no Chrome a read says `render.status: "unavailable"` and Tiresias never downloads one. The setting is called `rendering`, not `render`, so no MCP parameter shares a setting's name (W-29). `translator` (`config.json`: `{ "command": [full path, ...args], "timeout_seconds": 1 to 60, default 20 }`) is set with `tiresias translator set`, with the scanner's checks (a full path, no `..`, at most 31 arguments, no control characters, the program must exist), refused in private mode, and run as `<command> [args...] <source> <target>` with no shell, in its own process group, on macOS and Linux only. Both are listed in `tiresias settings`; no MCP tool reads or changes either.
- Save page (v0.8): `saving` (`off`, the default; `ask`) is set with `tiresias settings set saving ...` or `config.json`; **no environment variable** overrides it (an agent starting a CLI with its own environment could otherwise turn it on in passing). `max_saved_mb` (default 100) and at most 1,000 snapshots per profile: a save over either is refused (`over_cap`), never evicting an older one. `keep_saved_days` (default 90): `clear` and the daily trim remove older snapshots, leftover temporary folders over a day old, and the oldest snapshots while over a lowered `max_saved_mb`. `saved/` is not counted by `max_disk_mb`; `clear --all` and `--older-than` leave snapshots alone (`saved delete --all` removes them). An id is the 64-character hex id or a unique prefix of at least 8. Every `saved` command acts on the current profile. `saved verify` says `ok` only when the id, the signature, every file hash and every quote check out, and says when the reader's version differs from the one that made the snapshot.
- Tamper-proof receipts (v0.8): `anchoring` (`off`, the default; `on`) and `anchor_calendars` (1 to 8 `https://` addresses, comma-separated on the command line; default `a.pool.opentimestamps.org`, `b.pool.opentimestamps.org`, `a.pool.eternitywall.com`, `ots.btc.catallaxy.com`), CLI or `config.json`, no environment variable. `anchor` and `anchor upgrade` refuse while `anchoring` is off and in private mode; `anchor status`, `receipts prove` and `receipts verify` work either way (they send nothing). `anchor` exits 0 when it did its job (including nothing new), 1 when no calendar accepted the batch. Upgrades contact a pending attestation's calendar only when it is on the list or one the default pools hand out (`*.calendar.opentimestamps.org`, `*.calendar.eternitywall.com`, `*.calendar.catallaxy.com`), https only. A calendar failure is a fixed code (`network`, `timeout`, `too_large`, `refused`, `bad_answer`, `http_<status>`). `receipts prove` takes a `record_fingerprint` (with or without `sha256:`, or a unique prefix of at least 8 hex characters) or a snapshot id; without `--out` it prints the bundle (the record inside `untrusted_content`), with `--out` it writes `<name>.proof.json`, `<name>.json` (the exact bytes whose SHA-256 is the fingerprint) and `<name>.json.ots`, never overwriting, so the official client checks it too (`ots verify <name>.json.ots`). `receipts verify` reports `offline_ok` and `proven: false` always, its message starts "NOT proven", and it exits 2 when the offline checks pass, 1 when one fails; it gives the Bitcoin block height and Merkle root to compare (both byte orders). `anchors/` is not counted by `max_disk_mb` and never removed by `clear`. No daemon: cron, for example `17 3 * * * /full/path/to/tiresias anchor --text >> ~/.tiresias/anchor-cron.log 2>&1` (one line per profile with `--profile`).
- Memory ceiling (v0.9, W-33): `tiresias mcp` reads its own heap in use after every full garbage collection (and every second, at 1.5 times the ceiling); over `TIRESIAS_MEMORY_MB` (default 1024; 256 to 65536; `off` to skip; anything else stops `mcp` with a message naming the variable) it prints `Tiresias stopped: the MCP server reached its memory ceiling (<N> MB, TIRESIAS_MEMORY_MB). ...` on standard error, records `tool: "server"`, `event: "memory_ceiling"`, `memory_mb` in history (not in private mode; the daily log lists it under "Server stopped") and exits 1 through the usual shutdown. No `--max-old-space-size`: that flag would also lift every worker's own heap cap. Other commands run without it. Docker: `Dockerfile` and `docs/docker.md`.

---

## 7. Versions

| Version | Theme | Closes |
|---|---|---|
| **v0.1.0** | Honest reader. Ladder, shield, receipts, changed-since, walls, price tags, safe fetching, MCP + CLI. | N-01 to N-06, N-08 to N-10, W-01, W-11 |
| **v0.2.0** | Signed and tidy. Web Bot Auth signing, key directory, Cloudflare registration, daily log view, private mode, storage limits, PDFs. | N-07, W-09, W-13, W-14, W-15, W-17 |
| **v0.3.0** | Memory. Diffs, shared cache, handoff and resume, needs report, watchlist, Safe Browsing. | W-02, W-03, W-06, W-12, W-16, W-19 |
| **v0.4.0** | Permission. Permission slips, permission-gated forms, profiles. | W-04, W-05, W-18 |
| **v0.5.0** | Dangerous files. Every file named by its first bytes, risky ones flagged to the human and never run; optional bring-your-own scanner. | W-20, W-21 |
| **v0.5.1** | Review fixes and structure. The 2026-10-20 structure review (Banks and four Opus reviewers, `reviews/2026-10-20-structure-review.md`): slips gated on a real person, five cheap safety fixes, default politeness, engine split, private mode fails closed in the store, reader mode done right, ask for markdown first, repo hygiene ahead of the v1.0 launch (CI, SECURITY.md, tool annotations, exit codes, README), register and README drift fixed. | W-22, W-23; N-02, N-06 and N-10 gaps closed; W-05 hardened |
| **v0.6.0** | Inside files, plus browser basics. Listing what is inside a zip, reading text out of Office files, reading calendar invites as structured events (the "Not in v0.5" items of section 4.4); tabs and back/forward, site settings per origin, the downloads shelf, one settings view, and a fast first read. Built as three parallel tracks (section 9, 2026-10-24). | W-24, W-25, W-27, W-29, W-30 |
| **v0.7.0** | JavaScript-only pages, and translate. Revisits the 2026-10-23 walls and hidden-text decision (best effort vs "any password box or CAPTCHA is a wall") once rendering can see what is visible. | W-10, W-26 |
| **v0.8.0** | Tamper-proof receipts, and save page. | W-08, W-28 |
| **v0.9.0** | Hardening and a private launch rehearsal; risky settings need an all-caps confirmation; the MCP server under its own memory ceiling, with Docker optional; a site's echo of a secret withheld, not masked. | W-32, W-33 |
| **v1.0.0** | Public launch quality: docs, demo, tests, Codex-reviewed, verified on Cloudflare. Supported on macOS and Linux; Windows not yet (PDFs cannot be read there, and the scanner and translator do not run there) (section 9, 2026-10-31). The Codex review logs (`docs/reviews/`) stay private (section 9, 2026-10-31). | W-09 verified (2026-11-01) |
| **v1.0.1** | Bug clearing: a fresh hunt over the launched code; every finding fixed with a test or designed away (Travis, 2026-10-31). | 17 audit findings (section 10) |
| **v1.1.0** | Security deep dive (Travis, 2026-11-01): break every SECURITY.md promise end to end, an attack lab in the suite, supply chain and release integrity, key rotation, deeper fuzzing. Scope: `docs/v1.1/scope.md`. | 21 audit findings, key rotation, the attack lab, release provenance (section 10) |
| **v1.2.0** | Compliance deep dive (Travis, 2026-11-01). Scope to be written. | to be set |
| **v1.3.0** | Money. Paying with guardrails, built on the hardened and compliance-checked base (Travis, 2026-11-01). | W-07 |

Every version came in this order before launch (section 9, 2026-10-20). After launch: v1.0.1 bug clearing, v1.1 security, v1.2 compliance, v1.3 payments (section 9, 2026-11-01).

v1.0 is not tagged while any finding deferred to the compute-day sweep is open: each is fixed with a test or designed away, and nothing launches as a known bug. After launch, a v1.0.1 bug-clearing release comes before v1.1 (Travis, section 9, 2026-10-31).

Versioning follows semantic versioning (MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH).

---

## 8. Non-goals

Things Tiresias will not do, in any version:

- Solve, bypass or outsource CAPTCHAs.
- Fake a human browser fingerprint or rotate identities to avoid blocks.
- Ignore robots.txt or a site's stated agent rules.
- Hold the owner's passwords.
- Move money without a permission slip and a budget.
- Send the owner's data to Raibit or anyone else.
- Guarantee that every wall or every piece of hidden text is recognized. Walls and hidden-text flags are a courtesy and an advisory, decided by HTML heuristics on unrendered reads and measured by the browser, within a named set of techniques, on rendered ones (2026-10-31); the guarantees are that Tiresias never logs in, solves, pays or submits a refused form, and that every word from a page is returned as untrusted data (2026-10-23).

---

## 9. Decisions

| Date | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 2026-10-13 | Name: Tiresias. | The blind seer: a browser with no eyes that sees the web better. Chosen over "Oedipus." |
| 2026-10-13 | Success = learning the full open-source process. 10 stars is the bar; contributors are the prize. | The process is the win; contributors let it outlive the launch. |
| 2026-10-13 | v0.x reads price tags but never pays. | Money code is where things go wrong; it waits for permission slips, budgets and review. |
| 2026-10-13 | Signing uses Cloudflare's published Web Bot Auth library, not our own crypto. | Don't write your own cryptography. |
| 2026-10-15 | TypeScript on Node.js. | Official MCP SDK and Cloudflare's signing library are both TypeScript. |
| 2026-10-15 | History doubles as research data; the tool helps write its own wants and needs (W-11, W-12). | Travis: history "will help to analyze to see what else is needed." Real usage beats guessing. |
| 2026-10-15 | Daily logs are a view generated from history, not a second stored copy. | Travis: "whichever would be less disk space in the end." |
| 2026-10-15 | Chrome is the feature reference; private mode is local-only and never hides identity from sites. | Travis is "thinking about Chrome functions"; honesty to sites (principle 1) still wins. |
| 2026-10-16 | A 401/403 on a discovery probe path (llms.txt, well-known files) means "that door is unavailable", not a wall. The main page request always runs full wall detection on its own response. | A protected llms.txt is not a challenge to the page. A site that blocks the page itself still gets a `wall` and a handoff. Treating every probe 403 as a wall would stop reads on sites that simply deny dotfile paths. Real challenges, CAPTCHAs and 402 on a probe still stop the whole call. |
| 2026-10-16 | The cooldown store is a single append-only file, `cooldowns.jsonl`. `cooldowns.jsonl` is never compacted or deleted by Tiresias (not by `clear`, not by `clear --all`); it is a few lines per 429/503. Deleting it by hand is safe only when no Tiresias process is running. | Rotation, locking and then compaction all lost cooldowns under races (Codex rounds 3 to 5 and 11). Lines are appended only on a 429/503 and only when the deadline is later than known, so growth is tiny. Correctness beats tidiness. (Replaces the earlier plan that `clear` would tidy it.) |
| 2026-10-16 | On robots.txt: 401, 403 and 404 mean no rules (RFC 9309); a challenge (cf-mitigated header or CAPTCHA page markers) is a wall; 402 is a payment demand; 429 and 5xx stop the call and start a cooldown. | Follows RFC 9309 for plain 4xx while never treating a payment demand or a rate limit as permission. A site that blocks the page itself still gets a wall from the page request. |
| 2026-10-16 | Untrusted content is parsed and scanned inside a worker thread under a hard time budget (default 5 s). On timeout or cancellation the worker is terminated and the call returns `page_too_complex`, saving nothing. | Slow parsing paths in hostile pages kept appearing one at a time (regexes, quadratic copies), and synchronous code cannot be interrupted by a deadline. Fixing the class beats chasing each case. The specific slow paths were fixed as well so normal pages stay fast. |
| 2026-10-16 | License: Apache-2.0, with a NOTICE file naming Raibit. | Travis chose it over MIT (chat, 2026-10-16). It includes a patent grant, which matters as Tiresias moves into signing and payments where large companies hold patents. It keeps Raibit's notice on copies and is the norm for infrastructure, including Cloudflare's Web Bot Auth library. |
| 2026-10-16 | The repo stays private until v1.0.0, the public launch. | Travis (chat, 2026-10-16): fine waiting for v1.0, which the version plan defines as public launch quality (docs, demo, tests, Codex-reviewed, verified on Cloudflare). Launch once, ready. |
| 2026-10-16 | The user agent links to `https://raibit.ai/tiresias/` until v1.0, then back to GitHub. | The repo is private until v1.0, so the GitHub link showed site owners a "not found" page. Travis: "we can eliminate the page once the repo is public." |
| 2026-10-16 | The synced-folder check errs toward refusing. A store counts as inside a sync root if EITHER its real path is under the root ignoring letter case and Unicode spelling (this also covers roots that do not exist yet) OR one of its existing parent folders is the root itself (same device and inode, read as BigInt; an identity of 0 is ignored). Known limit: a bind mount of a folder from INSIDE a sync root to somewhere else is not detected. | A wrong refusal costs the owner one choice of another folder; a wrong pass would copy the private key to the cloud. Codex rounds 17 to 19 found holes in each single test (case rules, missing roots, identity collisions); combining them with OR closes all the false passes found. |
| 2026-10-16 | The synced-folder check only judges plain full paths (absolute, with a drive on Windows, no `..`). Anything else is refused at `keys init`. | Codex rounds 22 and 23 kept finding ways that `..`, relative paths, drive-relative Windows paths and links combine into a wrong answer. Refusing those inputs removes the whole class; owners only have to write the folder out in full once. |
| 2026-10-16 | A PDF answer is accepted when it arrived complete over IPC, the in-flight memory check settles, one more check of the child succeeds under the cap, and the child's own peak memory up to the answer (maxRSS) is under the cap. A child that dies AFTER sending a complete answer does not undo it; a child that dies BEFORE answering fails the request at once. | Codex rounds 30 to 32 chased the order of the exit event against the last memory reading. The answer is the child's last act and its peak memory covers everything up to it, so a later death says nothing about the answer. Stating the rule ends a timing race that has no safety value. |
| 2026-10-15 | Spec is the source of truth, kept in the repo with per-version history. | One place contributors and agents read before changing anything. |
| 2026-10-16 | Expanded JSON text is capped at 5 MiB (`MAX_EXPANDED`, the same size as the fetch cap). Text past the cap is dropped and reported as `text_omitted` (a character count) in the result. | A 94 KB nested JSON expanded to about 48 MB of text and an equally large cache file and worker message. A cap that is counted in the result matches how `links_omitted` already works, so nothing is lost silently. |
| 2026-10-16 | The worker has a 512 MB old-generation heap limit (`TIRESIAS_WORKER_HEAP_MB` to change it). It is not an absolute ceiling: Node's own `--max-old-space-size` overrides it, and young-generation and external memory are counted separately. Running out of heap is reported as `page_too_complex`. | A capped 5 MiB page needs well under 100 MB to parse; 512 MB leaves wide room for real pages while a runaway page ends the worker and not the process. |
| 2026-10-16 | A page is saved to the cache only after the answer for this call has been built. One exception: a missing section (`error`) still saves the page, because the page itself was read fine. A `no-store` response removes any old copy at once, whether or not the call then succeeds. | A call that fails while building the answer (for example a search timeout) says "Nothing was saved", so it must save nothing. Corrected after Codex round 9. |
| 2026-10-16 | Private mode skips the page cache entirely: it neither reads nor writes the page cache or the discovery cache, and writes no history line and no receipts. It also does not trigger the daily automatic trim. | Reading the cache would reveal earlier visits ("unchanged since...") and writing it would record this one. Simple and honest beats clever: a private call always fetches fresh and leaves the store as it found it. |
| 2026-10-16 | Private mode still honors existing cooldowns and robots.txt, still sends the same honest user agent, and still RECORDS a Retry-After cooldown (host and deadline only, no path). This is the one thing a private call writes. | Obeying the site beats local privacy (principle 1 and 2). A cooldown is the site's rule, not our record of the visit, and dropping it would let a private call hammer a site that asked us to wait. |
| 2026-10-16 | Default storage limits: history 90 days, receipts 90 days, page cache 30 days, whole-store cap 200 MB, all overridable in `config.json`. Past the cap the oldest cached pages go first, then the oldest history and receipts day files. A trim runs automatically at most once a day, on the first normal call, and never touches cooldowns. | Long enough to retrace a quarter's work, short enough that the store stays small. Cached pages are the cheapest thing to rebuild, so they go first. The automatic trim must be safe to run beside other processes, so it never rewrites the cooldown file. |
| 2026-10-16 | History and receipts are stored one file per UTC day (`history/YYYY-MM-DD.jsonl`, `receipts/YYYY-MM-DD.jsonl`, the date being the UTC date of the record's own timestamp), append only. Retention and the disk cap only ever DELETE whole day files (and whole cache files); Tiresias never rewrites a log. The v0.1 single files `history.jsonl` and `receipts.jsonl` are still read, never rewritten: age-based clear removes one only when its newest line is past retention, the cap may remove one whole as the oldest data, and `clear --all` removes them. | Codex round 11 found five holes (races, resurrected data, short writes, wrong eviction order) that all came from rewriting a log in place. Removing the moving part removes the whole class. The cost is a few more small files; the gain is that `--dry-run` is exact and a crash can never leave a half-written log. |
| 2026-10-16 | Private mode creates nothing. A private call neither reads nor writes the page cache, discovery cache, probe-body reuse or the shared robots cache, and does not create the store or any folder. The one allowed write is a Retry-After cooldown line (which creates the store folder if it does not exist yet). Reads, `clear --dry-run`, `log` and the history and receipts tools never create or chmod anything; a missing store means "nothing there". | Round 11 found a private probe body leaking into a later normal call, and private operations creating folders. A zero-trace promise has to cover memory shared between calls and the folders themselves. |
| 2026-10-16 | An export of the daily log (`log --to`) builds the whole text first, resolves the chosen folder once to its real path, writes an exclusive temp file inside it, then renames it to the final name there. It refuses a final name that is a symlink or a folder. A local process that can swap folders you own while an export runs is outside the threat model. | A real path plus an exclusive temp file closes the cheap attacks. Defending against someone who already controls your folders is not worth the complexity. |
| 2026-10-16 | Day files are named by UTC date. A day file is past retention when the end of its UTC day plus the retention period is before now. The daily log for a local day reads the UTC files that can overlap it and keeps records by their own timestamp converted to local time. Legacy files are unchanged. | The writer's timezone is not recorded, but each record's timestamp is. Local-date names made a timezone change hide visits and let cleanup delete data early (Codex round 12). UTC names make deletion safe after a timezone change or travel. |
| 2026-10-16 | Every use of the store's sub-folders (`cache/`, `history/`, `receipts/`) first checks that the folder is a real folder owned by this user, not a link; every deletion also checks the file is a plain regular file. A bad folder stops the call or the clear with a clear message and deletes nothing. | A link in place of `cache/` would let a delete land outside the store (Codex round 12). |
| 2026-10-16 | The only things a private call may write are the store root (created with mode 0700 only if missing, never chmodded) and a Retry-After cooldown line. It never creates `cache/` and never changes any folder or file mode. | The zero-trace promise has one named exception, and the exception must be as small as it can be. |
| 2026-10-16 | Recorded fields are bounded so one record cannot outgrow a page: agent 200 characters, purpose 1,000, URL 4,096, a logged receipt quote 8,000. A cut record says `cut: true`; the receipt handed to the agent is never cut. A failure to write history or receipts is printed to stderr and never fails the browsing call. Every append starts with a newline, so an unterminated last line can never swallow a record. The same bounds apply when records are read, so older oversized records stay readable. | Round 12: one 120 KB record made a history page unreadable over MCP, and an interrupted write could swallow the next record. Browsing must not depend on the log. |
| 2026-10-16 | The signing key lives at `<store>/keys/ed25519.private.jwk`: mode 0600 in a 0700 folder, created with exclusive create, replaced only with `--force` (temp file plus rename), validated like other store folders (no links, owned by us). It is never printed, logged or put in an error message. A key file readable by others is refused. | A private key is the identity. Exclusive create and a refusal to overwrite mean one slip cannot destroy it, and fixed error text means a damaged file's contents never leak. |
| 2026-10-16 | `keys init` refuses a store whose REAL path (links followed) is inside a sync root: any folder named exactly `OneDrive`, `OneDrive - ...`, `Dropbox` or `Google Drive`; `Library/CloudStorage`, `Library/Mobile Documents` (iCloud Drive); OneDrive's own storage folder `UBF8T346G9.OneDriveSyncClientSuite`; or under `%OneDrive%`, `%OneDriveConsumer%`, `%OneDriveCommercial%` when set. A path that merely contains the word (for example `...-OneDriveSyncClientSuite-OneDrive-noindex-...` as one long folder name) is allowed. The default `~/.tiresias` is outside OneDrive and stays there. | Synced folders copy the private key to other machines and the cloud. A substring match refused a perfectly unsynced scratch folder (found by Banks, 2026-10-16), so the check names real sync roots instead. |
| 2026-10-16 | Signatures live 60 seconds (`created` now, `expires` +60 s), with a fresh 64-byte nonce each, keyid = the JWK thumbprint, alg `ed25519`, tag `web-bot-auth`, covering `@authority` and `signature-agent`. | Cloudflare relies on short expiry for replay protection ("a minute is often sufficient") and does not check nonces; the nonce is cheap extra protection. |
| 2026-10-16 | Every outgoing request is signed: pages, robots.txt, discovery probes, and each redirect hop, re-signed for its own authority. Private mode signs too. | A signature for site A is useless and wrong on site B. Private mode hides nothing from sites (W-14). |
| 2026-10-16 | Signing never falls back silently. Signing is ON when a key exists and `signature_agent` is set; with neither or only one, behaviour is exactly as before. If it is on and cannot work (damaged key, bad config, unreadable file), the call fails with a clear message and sends nothing. | An agent that thinks it is signed but is not would be identified wrongly. Failing loudly is honest. |
| 2026-10-16 | `signature_agent_style` defaults to `quoted` (`Signature-Agent: "https://example.com"`), the form Cloudflare verifies today, and covers the plain `signature-agent` component. `dictionary` writes `sig1="https://example.com";type=directory` and covers `signature-agent;key="sig1"`, the form in the current IETF draft (now the working-group draft draft-ietf-webbotauth-httpsig-protocol, formerly draft-meunier-webbotauth-httpsig-protocol). | Cloudflare's docs (checked 2026-10-16) say they implement an older draft and reject the dictionary form, while the current draft says signers MUST send the dictionary. Cloudflare is the verifier we register with (W-09), so quoted is the default and the switch is one config line when they update. |
| 2026-10-16 | The key directory is hosted by us at `/.well-known/http-message-signatures-directory` with `Content-Type: application/http-message-signatures-directory+json`, and Cloudflare requires its RESPONSE to be signed (one signature per key; `("@authority";req)` set to the Host asked for; tag `http-message-signatures-directory`; keyid = thumbprint; `created`, `expires`). Decision (Travis, 2026-10-16): the response is PRE-SIGNED on this machine with `tiresias keys sign-directory`, so the private key never goes to the web server. The signature covers only the host, so the output (body plus fixed Content-Type, Signature and Signature-Input headers) can be served as static content. The signature expires (default 30 days, 1 to 90), so it must be renewed and re-uploaded every few weeks, before the printed expiry date. Cloudflare asks for nothing else on the response (its own example sends no cache header; `Cache-Control: max-age=86400` appears in the docs as an example only), and the live Cloudflare Research directory uses the same shape. | Verified against the docs and against Cloudflare Research's live directory response headers (2026-10-16). Keeping the key off the web host is the safer hosting plan. Superseded for Raibit's own directory on 2026-10-27 (signed live; row below). |
| 2026-10-16 | `signature_agent` must be an origin only (`https://host[:port]`, path empty or `/`, no query), in both header styles, and the header names the origin. | The current draft (draft-ietf-webbotauth-httpsig-protocol, section 5.5) defines `directory` discovery as an origin and tells verifiers to ignore other values. | 2026-10-16 | The public key is always DERIVED from the private seed; the stored `x` must match or the key is refused. `keys show`, `keys directory`, `sign-directory` and signing use only the derived key. | A damaged file with `x` set to the secret seed would otherwise print the private key as the "public" key (Codex round 16). |
| 2026-10-16 | Only a key file that is truly absent (ENOENT) means signing is off. Any other trouble with the key, its folder or the config (no permission, a link, wrong owner, readable by others, a file in the way) fails the call and sends nothing. The full storage checks run on EVERY signed request, not just when the key is first loaded; the parsed key is cached only while the file's stamp is unchanged. | A permission error must not quietly turn into unsigned traffic, and a later chmod or swapped folder must be caught at once (Codex round 16). `signed` counts a request only once it is actually written to the connection. |
| 2026-10-16 | PDFs are read with Mozilla pdf.js (`pdfjs-dist` 6.3.289, Apache-2.0, exact pin, legacy Node build), as door `text` with `door_detail: "pdf"`. It is the only parser for the job that is widely used and reviewed; writing our own would be worse. | PDF is a large, hostile format. Using the library every browser-grade viewer relies on, and locking it down, is safer than anything we could write. |
| 2026-10-16 | Travis approved installing pdf.js (2026-10-16) although its SkillSpector scan came back CRITICAL, after Banks checked the flags by hand and judged them false positives (binary character-map files and minified code). The install is locked down: `.npmrc` sets `ignore-scripts=true` (no install script runs), and the optional native `@napi-rs/canvas` is kept out by a local stub package (`stubs/no-canvas`) that `package.json` makes the only `@napi-rs/canvas` through `overrides`. (An earlier `omit=optional` setting also removed TypeScript's native compiler, so it was replaced.) | The scan flags came from data files and minified code, not behaviour. The lockdown removes the parts that could matter (native code, scripts). |
| 2026-10-16 | PDFs are read in a SEPARATE CHILD PROCESS (`pdfproc.ts`, `pdfchild.ts`), one per PDF, not in the worker thread. The parent starts it with `fork` (no shell), an empty environment, no stdin or stdout, `--max-old-space-size` from `TIRESIAS_WORKER_HEAP_MB`, and sends the bytes over IPC. At most 2 PDF children run at once. The HTML, markdown, JSON and search paths stay on the worker thread. | Codex round 28: a 488-byte PDF made the worker allocate about 320 MiB of ArrayBuffers, which a worker's heap limit does not count, so it could exhaust the whole process, including the MCP server. A separate process can only kill itself. Cost: a cold start of a fraction of a second per PDF. |
| 2026-10-16 | PDF memory watching FAILS CLOSED (Banks's rule, Codex round 29). The parent reads the child's real memory (resident set size) every 50 ms and SIGKILLs it above `TIRESIAS_PDF_MEMORY_MB` (default 1024). Linux reads `VmRSS` in kB from `/proc/<pid>/status` (explicit units, no page size assumed); macOS and BSD run `ps -o rss= -p <pid>` (no shell, empty environment). Before any child is started the parent probes its OWN memory the same way; if that fails (Windows, no `ps`, no `/proc`) PDFs are not read and the answer says so, while web pages are unaffected. **Windows therefore cannot read PDFs in v0.2.** A probe that fails or answers nonsense during a read kills the child and ends that request with a fixed message; it never raises an unhandled error or stops the server. | Process separation alone does not stop a PDF from exhausting the machine's memory. If memory cannot be watched, reading a PDF is not safe, so it is not done. |
| 2026-10-16 | The PDF child ends with its parent even when it is stuck in a loop that never yields: a tiny watchdog thread inside the child (`parentwatch.ts`, its own event loop) checks every 200 ms whether `process.ppid` changed from its starting value and, if so, SIGKILLs the child. The parent also kills its children on a normal exit, and the child exits when its IPC channel closes. The other limits are the time budget (`TIRESIAS_PARSE_BUDGET_MS`, SIGKILL at the deadline) and caller cancellation. | The `disconnect` event needs the child's main loop, which a busy parser may never give back; a separate thread does not. |
| 2026-10-16 | A PDF answer is size-checked twice: the child replaces an answer over 16 MB of JSON with a tiny "too large" one before sending (`capDone`), and the parent checks again. Any crash, signal, out of memory, odd or oversized answer, or timeout is `page_too_complex` and nothing is saved. | The check must happen before the allocation it protects, not after. |
| 2026-10-16 | pdf.js options: bytes passed as `data` (never a URL); `isEvalSupported: false`, `disableFontFace: true`, `useSystemFonts: false`, `disableAutoFetch`, `disableStream`, `disableRange`, `useWorkerFetch: false`, `enableXfa: false`, `stopAtErrors: false`, `verbosity: 0`; character maps, standard fonts, WASM and colour profiles are read from the installed package folder by file path. Console output is silenced while a PDF is read, because pdf.js prints warnings with `console.log` and a worker's stdout is the MCP stream. Text only: nothing is rendered, no JavaScript or action runs, no annotation or link is read (links in PDFs are ignored, not listed), no attachment is extracted, no form is touched. | Nothing is fetched, installed or run. Character maps load from disk, so CJK text works offline; the helper data was not skipped. |
| 2026-10-16 | A PDF is found by the media type alone (parameters dropped, case ignored) being exactly `application/pdf`, or by a body starting `%PDF-` when the type is missing, `application/octet-stream` or `binary/octet-stream`. An explicit `text/html`, `text/plain` or any other type is never a PDF, whatever the body starts with, and keeps its decoded body. The raw bytes are kept for PDFs (the text decoder is skipped). A PDF over the fetch cap (5 MB, `TIRESIAS_MAX_BYTES`) fails with a message that names the cap and, when the site sent it, the size; it is never cut, because a cut PDF cannot be read. | Counted, not silently truncated. |
| 2026-10-16 | One section per page, titled "Page N", with section N = page N, so a receipt's `section` is the page number. At most 500 pages are read; the rest are counted in `pdf_pages_omitted` (with `pdf_pages` the total). Total text is capped at 5 MiB (`MAX_EXPANDED`); text cut is counted in `text_omitted`, which is a lower bound (marked `text_omitted_at_least`) when whole pages were never read. The title comes from the PDF's metadata, cut at 200 characters. The "Page N" headings are labels Tiresias makes, not text from the PDF, so a section marked `generated` never gets a receipt for its heading (in the outline, section or question answers); every PDF receipt quote is an exact piece of the extracted text (or the metadata title). All of it is inside `untrusted_content`, and page text is scanned for phrases aimed at an AI (`ai_directed_instruction`); the hidden-text heuristics do not apply to PDFs. | Same bounds as HTML. No new envelope field was needed beyond the two counts. |
| 2026-10-16 | `PARSER_VERSION` is 7. Version 6 was used for the first PDF reader; 7 rebuilds PDF caches made before generated page headings were marked and before omitted text was counted exactly. | A 304 or an identical body would otherwise keep reusing a stale parse (`Page N` receipts, wrong counts). |

They are bounded like the other fields, on write and on read: a value that is not a known code or a whole number from 0 to 1,000 is left out and the record says `cut: true`. Older records stay valid. | The needs report needs these facts and they cost a few bytes a line. `llms_txt` was added beyond the plan because llms.txt is not a ladder code (a site with only llms.txt has best door `text`), and without it the report would call those sites doorless. |
| 2026-10-17 | The needs report is built from history when asked and never stored. Window: the last 30 days by default. Each pattern is a top list of 20 rows with exact record counts. At most 10,000 distinct groups (sites, pages, questions) are tracked per pattern; records past that are still counted and reported as `untracked`. "Visited often" is 3 or more visits in the window. A site is judged doorless only when some visit looked at its doors (a `read` or `doors` call) and none saw anything above page text or an llms.txt. "A better door existed" compares the door used with the best advertised one on the ladder (mcp, api, feed, structured, text) for reads where both are known. "Read most often" counts successful reads (ok or unchanged) of the same URL, twice or more. Rows are paged at 40 KB. | Bounded memory and exact counts, so a long history cannot stall the report or silently shrink it. Fixed definitions so two runs over the same history agree. |
| 2026-10-17 | Needs report suggestions are fixed templates with counts, one per pattern that showed up: walls name W-06, empty questions suggest a new want (search by meaning) and name W-10, better doors name N-01, rereads name W-03 and W-16, doorless sites name no ID (a new want to ask site owners for a door). The report never edits SPEC or anything else. | A person accepts or rejects each one. Fixed text keeps web-derived words out of the top level. |
| 2026-10-17 | W-02 diffs work on units: each section heading (not a generated PDF "Page N" label) and each block (paragraph, list item, at most 4,000 characters by the reader). Equal text is an equal unit; units are numbered by their text instead of hashed, which is the same comparison without hash work. The common start and end are trimmed, then Myers' diff runs on the rest. Caps: 5,000 units per side, 2,000 changed units, 2 MiB of changed text; past any cap the result is `changes_omitted` with a fixed message to read the whole page. A removed unit's `section` is its number on the old page; each change also carries its section heading for context. | Block granularity matches receipts (an added block is a whole extracted block, so its receipt quote is exact). Myers' memory is about the square of the edit count, so the edit cap bounds memory as well as time. |
| 2026-10-17 | The diff runs in the worker under the parse time budget, after the new page is parsed and BEFORE the cached copy is replaced. `check` always diffs a changed page; `read` diffs only with `changes: true`, and a plain read of a changed page does no diff work and keeps no list. There is nothing to compare against (a fixed message, no `changes`) on a first visit, in private mode, when the page is `no-store`, when the kept copy came from an older reader version, or when the page is now read from another source (for example an llms.txt appeared). A 304 or an identical body is unchanged and needs no diff. | Diffing is CPU work over untrusted text. Comparing across reader versions or sources would list changes the site never made. |
| 2026-10-17 | The list of changes is kept inside the new cached page (`changes`, with the time it was found and the time of the copy it was compared with), so later pages can be read: on an unchanged page, `check` with `page` (or `read` with `changes` and `page`) returns that page of the kept list. The parsed form did not change, so `PARSER_VERSION` stays 7: the field is optional, v0.2 ignores it, and a v0.2 entry simply has no list. | Without keeping it, page 2 of a long list could never be read: the first check replaces the copy it was compared with. One cached file per page, replaced whole, adds no moving part. |
| 2026-10-17 | `check` now writes receipts to the receipts log when it returns added blocks (it returned no quotes before). The no-store message on `check` no longer says the new version was cached. | Every quote handed to an agent is logged (N-03). The old text was wrong for no-store pages. |
| 2026-10-17 | Bookmarks are one JSON file each, `bookmarks/<name>.json` (`name`, `url`, `watch`, `added_at`), in a store sub-folder validated like the others (real folder, ours, not a link; files opened non-blocking, without following links, checked regular and at most 16 KB). Add is an exclusive create, remove deletes the one file, and changing `watch` writes an exclusive temp file and renames it over that one bookmark: if two processes change the same bookmark at once the last rename wins, and the file is always whole. Names are `[a-z0-9-]{1,64}`; a bad name is refused with fixed text that does not repeat it. URLs must be http or https without credentials and at most 2,048 characters (refused, never cut). The cap is 1,000, checked before each add (two adds at the same moment can pass it by one each). Damaged files are counted in the list, not fatal. The list is returned inside `untrusted_content`, since an agent may have copied a URL from a page. | No shared file to rewrite, so no locks. A bookmark is tiny, so the cap is about keeping the list readable, not disk. |
| 2026-10-17 | `watch` checks watched bookmarks one after another, as `check` does (robots, cooldowns, signing), and records each as a history line with tool `watch` and the bookmark name as the purpose. It lists the bookmarks that changed (against a kept copy), with what changed, and counts the rest: unchanged, no baseline (first visit or private), walled, skipped by a cooldown (refused with a wait), and failed (any other refusal, error or price tag). The walled, failed and waiting ones are also NAMED in a top-level `attention` list: bookmark name, `outcome` (`walled`, `failed`, `waiting`), the fixed `status`, the fixed handoff `reason` for a wall, and `retry_after_seconds` when the site asked for a wait. Their URLs and, for a wall, the page to open sit in `untrusted_content.attention_urls`. At most 100 are named; the rest are counted in `counts.attention_omitted`. Unchanged and no-baseline bookmarks stay counts only. (Changed 2026-10-17 at the coordinator's request, before Codex review: a walled bookmark is exactly the one a person needs to act on, so a count alone hid it.) Pages: each page checks the next 10 watched bookmarks, so a page is its own bounded run and no run state is stored. The CLI without `--page` checks all. Each bookmark shows only the START of `check`'s own page 1 of changes, as much as fits about 4,000 characters, with `changes_pages` counted at `check`'s page size and `changes_more` when there is more: the full list is `check <url>` from page 1, so no page number in a watch reply ever differs from `check`'s (Codex round 36). A paged reply still over the 60 KB budget drops, in order and each counted: whole lists of changes from the last changed bookmark backwards (`changes_trimmed`), then attention URLs (`attention_urls_omitted`), then attention names (`attention_omitted`). Attention URLs are also capped at 20,000 characters in any reply. The CLI's all-bookmarks run (no `--page`) is not held to the MCP budget. Tiresias has no daemon or scheduler: the owner runs `watch` from cron or launchd. | Bounded work per call and no stored state. A scheduler inside Tiresias would be a moving part that runs without a person asking. |
| 2026-10-17 | Private mode and bookmarks: bookmarks are the owner's saved list, not a record of a visit, but a private session must not create the store, so in private mode adding, removing and changing bookmarks are refused with a clear message. Listing reads only. `watch` in private mode runs every check privately: fresh, nothing compared (no cache is read), nothing recorded. | Keeps the private promise (creates nothing) without hiding the owner's list from them. |

A no-store answer (an identical 200 or a 304) never serves a kept list. (Codex round 36.) | Unknown is not zero. A site that asks that nothing be kept must not see an old list come back. |

It is parsed exactly like a fetched page of that type, in the worker or the PDF child under the same limits. NO request of any kind is made (no robots.txt, no probe), so the reply has no `signed` field and nothing is ever sent to the site, cookies and credentials included (Tiresias holds none). | 2026-10-17 | W-03: the store belongs to one owner, so in RFC 9111 terms it is a PRIVATE cache, and that owner's agents on one machine (Claude Code, Codex, Nova) count as one user. Therefore `Cache-Control: private` responses may be kept and reused across the owner's agents, and `s-maxage` (for shared caches only) is ignored. | A shared cache in the RFC sense serves many users; this one serves one person's agents. |
| 2026-10-17 | W-03 freshness, deliberately conservative (replaces the first RFC 9111 rule set, after Codex round 38 found five holes in it): instead of following RFC 9111 edge by edge, one simple form of permission counts. Only `Cache-Control: max-age=N` gives a lifetime; `Expires` and `Date` are never used (no date parsing at all). The lifetime is capped at 1 hour whatever max-age says. Age at the response is the `Age` header, which must be one plain whole number of at most 9 digits; the copy's age is counted from the moment the REQUEST STARTED, so the time the request took counts too. Any of these makes the cached copy not reusable until a full new 200 replaces it, stored as a sticky `reuse_blocked: true`: `no-store`, `no-cache` (any form), `max-age=0`, `max-age` given twice or not a plain whole number, a `Pragma` header (any value), a `Vary` naming anything but `accept`, `accept-encoding` or `user-agent`, a Cache-Control that does not parse as simple directives or is over 1,000 UTF-8 bytes, a Vary over 500 bytes, or an `Age` that is not one plain whole number. A 304 can never clear the block (it can add one) and never keeps an old deadline (Codex round 39): for a copy that was reusable, the deadline is recomputed only from the 304's own headers under the same rules (its own `max-age` and `Age`, counted from that request's start); a 304 with no valid `max-age` leaves the copy with no freshness (its deadline becomes that request's start, already past) until a later response gives one. A copy that never had a lifetime is not made reusable by a 304. No heuristic freshness; `s-maxage` is ignored; `private` is fine (see the row above). A copy made by an older reader version is never served fresh. | Every hole Codex found was an edge of a rule we did not need. An hour of reuse is the saving agents need; asking the site again costs one conditional request. Fewer rules, no dates, sticky blocks: the whole class goes away. |
| 2026-10-17 | W-03 serving a fresh copy (revised after Codex rounds 38 and 39): the cached page records `redirected` (true when the fetch that produced or last revalidated it followed any redirect at all, taken from the fetch itself, not inferred from URLs: an A to B to A chain ends where it started but is still a redirect), and only a copy with `redirected: false` (a missing field counts as not reusable) that was read from the page itself (source URL = asked URL, so not llms.txt or a markdown twin) is ever served fresh; the rules checked for the asked URL are then the rules of the page served. robots.txt and the asked origin's cooldown are checked first, as for any call; if this process does not know robots.txt yet it fetches it (one request per process per hour). The coordinator allowed serving without robots when it is unknown, since serving from the cache sends nothing; we kept the stricter order because it is the existing path and adds no moving part. Page doors come only from the discovery cache. Each reply says `cache` (`fresh`, `revalidated`, `refetched`; absent when no copy took part) and, inside `untrusted_content`, `cached_by` and `cached_at` for `fresh` and `revalidated`. | 2026-10-17 | Probe-body reuse is removed (Codex round 39; replaces the round 38 row that only bypassed it for `fresh`). A discovery probe's llms.txt body used to be kept in memory for 30 seconds so a read right after could skip fetching it again. Every page read now sends its own request. Chosen over a per-call map because it removes the moving part outright and costs at most one extra llms.txt request per call that probed (a cold discovery cache). It closes both holes Codex found: a kept body that came through a redirect skipped the target site's cooldown and rules, and a kept body could roll content back after a `fresh` read. `fresh: true` and a resume retry therefore always hear from the site in this call. | Less memory shared between calls is less to get wrong; one request is cheap. |
| 2026-10-17 | An error envelope copies the caller's URL only when it is at most 2,048 bytes; otherwise `url` is empty and `url_omitted: true` says so. Other error texts that echo caller input are bounded too (a bad `since` date is cut at 40 characters, like a bad `day`); ticket ids, bookmark names and file paths are never echoed. | A fallback that can be as large as its input is not a fallback. |
| 2026-10-17 | Every budget that protects a reply's size counts UTF-8 bytes of the JSON sent, not UTF-16 characters: the MCP 100 KB last guard, the 60 KB total, and the outline, link, section, passage, history, receipts, log, needs, bookmarks, handoffs and watch pages. The daily log splits an over-long line into pieces of 40,000 / 6 characters (one character is at most 6 bytes once escaped). Record bounds that limit fields (agent 200, purpose 1,000 characters and so on) stay in characters; the pages around them are what is measured in bytes. Header size limits in freshness count bytes too. (Codex round 38: a handoffs reply with Chinese questions was 109 KB.) A fresh serve writes nothing. A test runs two real processes against one entry, and fails when the write is made non-atomic. If two processes write the same entry, the last rename wins. | 2026-10-17 | W-19 sources (Travis): the default list for everyone is Phishing.Database (MIT license, no key), its `phishing-domains-ACTIVE.txt` (one domain per line, about 11 MB and 392,000 domains on 2026-10-17; the 66 MB links file is not used). | 2026-10-17 | W-19 list storage: one file, `safety/phishing-domains.txt` (a JSON header line with source URL, ETag, Last-Modified, download and confirmation times and the count, then the hosts sorted, one per line), written to an exclusive temp file and renamed; plus `safety/attempt.json` (time and outcome of the last download try). `safety/` is a store sub-folder validated like the others, files opened without following links and non-blocking, checked regular and ours. A download is due when the copy was last confirmed over 24 hours ago, except within an hour of a failed try. It is conditional (If-None-Match, If-Modified-Since): a 304 rewrites the file with a new confirmation time. It goes through the polite fetcher like every request (robots.txt, waits, honest user agent, signed), capped at 32 MB (over it, the download fails with a message saying so and the last good copy stays), with its own timeout of 30 seconds (120 seconds for `safety update`). Concurrent calls in one process share one download, which runs outside every call: it is not tied to the cancellation or deadline of the call that started it, and each waiting call waits only until its own cancellation or deadline, while the download goes on for the others (Codex round 42). The download is never aborted: it always runs to completion under its own fixed timeouts (30 seconds to download, the normal worker budget to parse), so any call that joins it, even after the call that started it gave up, gets its result. Accepted cost (Codex round 43 raised it, round 44 decided): if a CLI call gives up while a download is stalled, the process may take up to the download timeout to exit on its own; a long-running MCP server is unaffected. (Round 43 stopped the download when its last waiting call left; round 44 found a call joining that stopped download got no list, so the mechanism was removed rather than patched.) Two processes may both download, and the last rename wins. A list with no domains is refused. | One file per item, replaced whole, no locks. The header and the hosts are one file so they can never disagree. A failed try is recorded so a list host that is down is not asked on every call (each CLI run is its own process). |
| 2026-10-17 | W-19 parsing and matching: the downloaded text is untrusted, so it is normalized in the worker under the parse budget: each line trimmed and lower-cased, blank and `#` lines skipped, a trailing dot dropped, plain hosts (`a-z`, `0-9`, `-`, `_`, `.`, two labels or more) kept as they are, anything else passed through the WHATWG URL parser (so IDN names become punycode) and kept only if the result is a plain host; lines with spaces, `/`, `@`, `:`, `%` and the like are rejected and counted. The result is sorted and unique. A lookup is a binary search over the stored file's own bytes, for the URL's host (already lower case and punycode from `new URL`) and each parent domain down to two labels (`a.b.example.com`, `b.example.com`, `example.com`, never `com`); an IP address is matched as is. Reading the stored file back checks every character, the order and the count, and a file that fails is treated as damaged (no list) and replaced by the next download. Measured on the real list (2026-10-17, Apple silicon): normalize 176 ms in the worker, load and index 74 ms on the main thread (once per process, and again only when the file changes), about 13 MB of memory (the file's bytes plus a 4-byte offset per domain; a read process peaked at 107 MB resident against 87 MB with Safe Browsing off), a lookup about 3 microseconds; a `safety update` process peaked at 260 MB resident for 1.3 seconds. Known, bounded mitigation (Codex round 41): a built-in set of about 90 common multi-label public suffixes (country second levels like `co.uk`, `com.au`, `co.jp`, and shared hosts like `github.io`, `pages.dev`, `herokuapp.com`, `blogspot.com`, `appspot.com`, `000webhostapp.com`) is never matched as a PARENT domain: a list entry equal to one is kept and still flags its own exact host (an entry `com.es` flags `com.es`, never `shop.com.es`), and is counted (`suffix_entries` in the file header and in `safety status`). Exact hosts below a suffix (`evil.github.io`) still match, with their subdomains. (Codex round 43 replaced the round 41 rule that dropped such entries.) Any two-label name whose first label is a generic second level (`com`, `co`, `net`, `org`, `gov`, `edu`, `ac`, `or`, `ne`, `go`, `mil`, `nom`, `gob`, `gen`) AND whose second label is a two-letter country code counts as a suffix too, so `com.es` or `gob.mx` on the list never flags everything under it (Codex round 42), while `gov.com` or `co.com`, ordinary registrable domains, are matched as usual (Codex round 43). It is not the full Public Suffix List (no new dependency), so a rarer suffix on the list would still flag its children. | A sorted byte buffer avoids 392,000 string objects (a Set would cost several times the memory). Two labels as the floor keeps one bad line from flagging a whole top-level domain, and the suffix set does the same for the common shared names where unrelated people register or host sites. |
| 2026-10-17 | W-19 when the check runs: before anything is sent. The asked URL is checked first, before robots.txt and before a copy is served from the shared cache (a fresh copy of a page whose site is now listed is not served). Then the fetcher checks every hop (redirects, robots.txt, probes) before it is sent. The list is checked once per host per call (it lists domains); Web Risk is asked once per distinct URL per call (the full URL of each hop, so a redirect to a listed path on a host already checked is still stopped), and checks of one host or URL at the same moment share one answer. The list download itself is not checked. A resume from a page a person provided is not checked, because nothing is requested. (Changed 2026-10-17 at the coordinator's request, before Codex review: the first build asked Web Risk once per host, which missed path-level listings after a same-host redirect.) | Web Risk lists URLs and paths, not only sites, so every URL actually requested is the thing to check; the in-memory verdict cache keeps repeats free. A new status (not `refused` with a reason) lets an agent tell "the list thinks this is phishing, you may go on" from "no" without parsing text. |

A bad setting is different: an unknown value for `safe_browsing`, `web_risk_key_file`, `TIRESIAS_SAFE_BROWSING` or the test URLs, or a key file Tiresias refuses, fails the call with fixed text and sends nothing. | The list host being down is not the owner's fault and must not stop work. It is made on the first `slip grant`, written whole to a temp file and hard-linked to its name (two first grants at once end with one key). It is used only for slips, never for Web Bot Auth. Each slip file holds its fields plus `sig`, an Ed25519 signature (base64url) over a canonical byte form: the JSON array `["tiresias-permission-slip", v, id, profile, action, site, path, created_at, expires_at, uses, note]`, a fixed order with a fixed tag. A slip is used only when its file name matches its id, it has exactly these fields, each is valid, its profile is the current one, its life is at most 30 days, and the signature verifies; otherwise it is `damaged`: counted, never used. With no slip key, every slip is damaged. Any other trouble with the key (readable by others, a link, damaged) fails loudly for granting, listing and using slips. It does NOT block unrelated housekeeping (coordinator, Codex round 49): `clear` and the daily trim then skip the slip cleanup only, leave every slip as it is, say so in `slips_problem` (and the summary; the daily trim prints it to stderr), and still trim the cache, history, receipts and handoff tickets. **What signing protects against:** a slip file edited by hand (more uses, a wider path, a later end), a damaged file, and a slip copied from another profile (another key, and the profile name is inside the signed bytes). **What it does not:** a process running as the owner can read the slip key and sign any slip, or delete use files to give uses back. That is the same threat model as the whole store. | A slip is a permission, so a changed slip must stop working rather than grant more. A separate key keeps the web identity and the permission store apart, so neither can be used as the other. |

Anything at a use's name counts as taken, so an odd file can only take permission away. The use file holds the time and the caller's label. | 2026-10-17 | **What form submission guarantees** (W-04, coordinator after Codex round 54). (a) Nothing is sent without a valid, in-scope, unused permission slip that the owner granted. (b) Exactly one request, to the slip's origin and an allow-listed path; no retry and no redirect followed. (c) Never a password, file upload, payment-looking or CAPTCHA form. (d) The request sent is exactly the one previewed, apart from hidden values taken fresh from the site. (e) Tiresias does not show hidden values to the agent, and masks the site's echoes of them; this holds because every hidden value it sends is plain printable ASCII (`bad_hidden_value` otherwise), so an echo can only come back raw, form-urlencoded, percent-encoded or HTML-escaped, and every one of those is masked (values under 6 characters are not masked, as ordinary words would be). It does NOT guarantee how a particular server parses parameters: any HTTP client can send any values to a site, so a site's own server-side checks remain the site's job. Tiresias closes the cheap, known cases (canonical name collisions, a query riding in from the page address) but does not model every framework. | Reviews kept finding one more backend's parameter rules (PHP name normalization, servlet query precedence, sites echoing tokens). Writing the scope down says what is promised and what is the site's own responsibility, so each new framework is judged against a stated line rather than an open one. |
